It was silly, really—my wife always used to say I fussed with table settings too much—but I don’t usually speak to people like that. It bothered me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other moments recently where you felt suddenly angry or lost control of your emotions?\n\nPatient: Well… the one that really sticks is that tablecloth business at the volunteer hall. One of the young fellows had it laid out crooked, and I just snapped at him. I don’t usually speak to people that way.\n\nIt’s funny, my wife used to straighten things like that before anyone even noticed—she had an eye for it. But no, I mean… that was what bothered me, losing my temper over something so small.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other episodes recently where you felt sudden anger or found yourself reacting more emotionally than usual, even over small things?\n\nPatient: I keep coming back to that tablecloth, I suppose. It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but one of the younger volunteers had it crooked on the table and I just… I snapped at him. Raised my voice more than I should have.\n\nI don’t usually talk to people like that. I mean, I’ve always liked things set properly—my wife used to tease me about it, though she was the one who could make a room look right in five minutes—but this was different. It was too much over something so small.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other times recently where you felt yourself getting upset or angry more easily than usual, even about small things?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think if there was another time, but honestly the one that keeps coming up is that tablecloth at the volunteer hall. One of the younger volunteers had it crooked and I just… I blew up at him. Over a tablecloth, of all things.\n\nMy wife used to have a real eye for that sort of thing—she could set a room right without making anyone feel foolish. I suppose that’s why it stuck with me. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not the younger ones. It bothered me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any other situations recently, outside of the volunteer hall incident, where you felt unusually emotional or reacted more strongly than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Outside of that… I’m trying to think. I mean, I’ve had little moments where things get under my skin, but the one that really brought me in was that business with the crooked tablecloth at the volunteer hall. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers, and that’s not like me.\n\nHe didn’t deserve it, really. It was just a tablecloth. My wife used to fuss with those things too—well, she made it look effortless—but I shouldn’t have spoken to him that way. That’s the part that bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable or angry in general, even when you're not thinking about the tablecloth incident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m angry all the time. It’s more… well, that tablecloth thing is what scared me, really. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over it being crooked, and I don’t usually speak to people like that.\n\nHe was just trying to help, you know. Young fellow, probably reminded me a little of one of the boys I used to see at church events years ago—always in a hurry, but meaning well. And there I was, raising my voice over something that shouldn’t have mattered that much. So maybe, yes, I suppose I’ve been easier to set off than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself being more easily annoyed or upset in general, even in situations that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though the one I keep circling back to is that day at the volunteer hall. It was just a crooked tablecloth, and I snapped at one of the younger volunteers like he’d done something terrible.\n\nFunny thing is, I used to help set up church suppers years ago, and there was always someone fussing with the linens—my wife had a knack for making it look nice without making a production of it. But me, I just… I blew up. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not someone who’s only trying to help. That’s what made me think something’s been off with me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you've been in a more irritable mood most days, even when nothing in particular is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: Most days… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, I have my moods like anybody. You get to my age and little things can bother you—noise, people rushing around, that sort of thing.\n\nBut what really made me think something wasn’t right was at the volunteer hall. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. Just a tablecloth. And I don’t usually talk to people that way. That’s what’s been sitting with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around angry all the time, no… but short-tempered, maybe. What keeps coming back to me is that business at the volunteer hall. I snapped at one of the younger fellows over a crooked tablecloth.

Most days… I’m not sure how to measure that. I don’t wake up looking for a fight or anything. But I suppose I have been easier to set off.

I don’t know if I’d call it most days, exactly. I mean, some days I’m just doing my regular things—well, regular as they are now—and I’m not thinking I’m annoyed.

I’m not sure I’d put it as “most days,” exactly… I don’t sit around feeling mad for no reason. But I know I’ve been touchier.

I don’t usually talk to people that way, and afterward I felt awful about it. So yes, I guess short-tempered is fair.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing in particular is bothering you, do you find yourself feeling irritable or easily annoyed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it most days, exactly. It was too much over something so small.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other times recently where you felt yourself getting upset or angry more easily than usual, even about small things?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think if there was another time, but honestly the one that keeps coming up is that tablecloth at the volunteer hall. One of the younger volunteers had it crooked and I just… I blew up at him. Over a tablecloth, of all things.\n\nMy wife used to have a real eye for that sort of thing—she could set a room right without making anyone feel foolish. I suppose that’s why it stuck with me. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not the younger ones. It bothered me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any other situations recently, outside of the volunteer hall incident, where you felt unusually emotional or reacted more strongly than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Outside of that… I’m trying to think. I mean, I’ve had little moments where things get under my skin, but the one that really brought me in was that business with the crooked tablecloth at the volunteer hall. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers, and that’s not like me.\n\nHe didn’t deserve it, really. It was just a tablecloth. My wife used to fuss with those things too—well, she made it look effortless—but I shouldn’t have spoken to him that way. That’s the part that bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable or angry in general, even when you're not thinking about the tablecloth incident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m angry all the time. It’s more… well, that tablecloth thing is what scared me, really. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over it being crooked, and I don’t usually speak to people like that.\n\nHe was just trying to help, you know. Young fellow, probably reminded me a little of one of the boys I used to see at church events years ago—always in a hurry, but meaning well. And there I was, raising my voice over something that shouldn’t have mattered that much. So maybe, yes, I suppose I’ve been easier to set off than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself being more easily annoyed or upset in general, even in situations that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though the one I keep circling back to is that day at the volunteer hall. It was just a crooked tablecloth, and I snapped at one of the younger volunteers like he’d done something terrible.\n\nFunny thing is, I used to help set up church suppers years ago, and there was always someone fussing with the linens—my wife had a knack for making it look nice without making a production of it. But me, I just… I blew up. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not someone who’s only trying to help. That’s what made me think something’s been off with me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you've been in a more irritable mood most days, even when nothing in particular is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: Most days… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, I have my moods like anybody. You get to my age and little things can bother you—noise, people rushing around, that sort of thing.\n\nBut what really made me think something wasn’t right was at the volunteer hall. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. Just a tablecloth. And I don’t usually talk to people that way. That’s what’s been sitting with me.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing specific is bothering you, do you find yourself feeling more irritable or on edge than usual throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s all day, no. I don’t sit around feeling mad for no reason, exactly. It’s more that something small can set me off before I’ve had a chance to catch myself.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. A tablecloth, of all things. My wife used to fuss with those at church dinners, you know, but she had a gentle way about it. Me, I raised my voice at the poor fellow, and I don’t usually talk to people that way. That’s what made me think, well… maybe I’m more on edge than I realize.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing specific is happening, do you find yourself feeling more irritable or short-tempered than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m walking around angry all the time, no… but short-tempered, maybe. What keeps coming back to me is that business at the volunteer hall. I snapped at one of the younger fellows over a crooked tablecloth.\n\nIt sounds foolish saying it out loud—a tablecloth. My wife used to be particular about those things too, especially for church suppers, but she never made anyone feel small over it. And there I was raising my voice like he’d done something terrible. I don’t usually talk to people that way, so… yes, I guess something in me has been quicker to flare up than usual.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling more easily annoyed or short-tempered most days, even when nothing in particular is going on?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’m not sure how to measure that. I don’t wake up looking for a fight or anything. But I suppose I have been easier to set off.\n\nLike I said, the thing that really bothered me was at the volunteer hall. One of the younger volunteers had put a tablecloth on crooked, and I just… blew up at him. Over that. A crooked tablecloth. My wife would’ve just straightened it and maybe made a joke, you know? I don’t usually talk to people that way, and afterward I felt awful about it. So yes, I guess short-tempered is fair.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing in particular is bothering you, do you find yourself feeling irritable or easily annoyed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it most days, exactly. A tablecloth. My wife used to notice things like that right away, you know, but she’d just fix it. I raised my voice, and I don’t usually talk to people that way. So… yes, I guess I’ve been more easily set off than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been in a more irritable mood most days, even when nothing specific is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “most days,” exactly… I don’t sit around feeling mad for no reason. So… yes, more irritable than I used to be, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling more irritable or short-tempered most days, even when nothing in particular is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I used to be. I don’t know that I’d say nearly every day, most of the day… that sounds stronger than what I mean. But I have been more on edge, more likely to snap.

That’s the part that bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more irritable or angry in general, even when you're not thinking about the tablecloth incident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m angry all the time. It’s more… well, that tablecloth thing is what scared me, really. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over it being crooked, and I don’t usually speak to people like that.\n\nHe was just trying to help, you know. Young fellow, probably reminded me a little of one of the boys I used to see at church events years ago—always in a hurry, but meaning well. And there I was, raising my voice over something that shouldn’t have mattered that much. So maybe, yes, I suppose I’ve been easier to set off than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself being more easily annoyed or upset in general, even in situations that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though the one I keep circling back to is that day at the volunteer hall. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, “down” is a big word, isn’t it? Some days I’m quieter, I suppose, but I don’t know that I’ve lost interest exactly.

The volunteer hall… well, I still go. Or I try to. It’s funny, they changed where they keep the coffee urns now, used to be in the back cupboard near the folding chairs, and nobody ever puts things back where they belong anymore. But no, I can’t really say I’ve stopped caring about it. I just… I don’t know. I haven’t thought of it in those terms.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day, exactly. I’m not sitting there angry from breakfast to supper. But I have been more short-tempered than I recognize in myself.

So maybe, yes, I suppose I’ve been easier to set off than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself being more easily annoyed or upset in general, even in situations that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though the one I keep circling back to is that day at the volunteer hall. It was just a crooked tablecloth, and I snapped at one of the younger volunteers like he’d done something terrible.\n\nFunny thing is, I used to help set up church suppers years ago, and there was always someone fussing with the linens—my wife had a knack for making it look nice without making a production of it. But me, I just… I blew up. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not someone who’s only trying to help. That’s what made me think something’s been off with me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you've been in a more irritable mood most days, even when nothing in particular is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: Most days… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, I have my moods like anybody. You get to my age and little things can bother you—noise, people rushing around, that sort of thing.\n\nBut what really made me think something wasn’t right was at the volunteer hall. I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. Just a tablecloth. And I don’t usually talk to people that way. That’s what’s been sitting with me.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing specific is bothering you, do you find yourself feeling more irritable or on edge than usual throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s all day, no. I don’t sit around feeling mad for no reason, exactly. It’s more that something small can set me off before I’ve had a chance to catch myself.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. A tablecloth, of all things. My wife used to fuss with those at church dinners, you know, but she had a gentle way about it. Me, I raised my voice at the poor fellow, and I don’t usually talk to people that way. That’s what made me think, well… maybe I’m more on edge than I realize.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing specific is happening, do you find yourself feeling more irritable or short-tempered than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m walking around angry all the time, no… but short-tempered, maybe. What keeps coming back to me is that business at the volunteer hall. I snapped at one of the younger fellows over a crooked tablecloth.\n\nIt sounds foolish saying it out loud—a tablecloth. My wife used to be particular about those things too, especially for church suppers, but she never made anyone feel small over it. And there I was raising my voice like he’d done something terrible. I don’t usually talk to people that way, so… yes, I guess something in me has been quicker to flare up than usual.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling more easily annoyed or short-tempered most days, even when nothing in particular is going on?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’m not sure how to measure that. I don’t wake up looking for a fight or anything. But I suppose I have been easier to set off.\n\nLike I said, the thing that really bothered me was at the volunteer hall. One of the younger volunteers had put a tablecloth on crooked, and I just… blew up at him. Over that. A crooked tablecloth. My wife would’ve just straightened it and maybe made a joke, you know? I don’t usually talk to people that way, and afterward I felt awful about it. So yes, I guess short-tempered is fair.\n\nDoctor: Even when nothing in particular is bothering you, do you find yourself feeling irritable or easily annoyed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it most days, exactly. I mean, some days I’m just doing my regular things—well, regular as they are now—and I’m not thinking I’m annoyed.\n\nBut what brought me in was that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. A tablecloth. My wife used to notice things like that right away, you know, but she’d just fix it. I raised my voice, and I don’t usually talk to people that way. So… yes, I guess I’ve been more easily set off than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been in a more irritable mood most days, even when nothing specific is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “most days,” exactly… I don’t sit around feeling mad for no reason. So… yes, more irritable than I used to be, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling more irritable or short-tempered most days, even when nothing in particular is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I used to be. I used to help set up those things all the time, and usually somebody would just straighten it and move on. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not someone just trying to help. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Nearly every day for most of the day… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not keeping a tally, you know.

But I have been snapping in ways that aren’t like me. That whole thing at the volunteer hall is what made me think something was off—I raised my voice at one of the younger fellows because the tablecloth was crooked. My wife always liked things set nicely, corners even and all that, but still… it was only a tablecloth. I don’t usually speak to people like that.

So yes, I suppose I have been more irritable.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling irritable nearly every day for most of the day, even when nothing in particular is going on to upset you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say nearly every day, most of the day… that sounds stronger than what I mean. But I have been more on edge, more likely to snap.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, was I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. A tablecloth, of all things. We were setting up, and years ago my wife and I used to help with those sorts of dinners, and she always had such a neat way of making things look proper without embarrassing anybody. And there I was raising my voice at this young fellow who was just trying to help.\n\nSo… not all day every day, no. But irritable enough that it worried me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like helping at the volunteer hall or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, “down” is a big word, isn’t it? Some days I’m quieter, I suppose, but I don’t know that I’ve lost interest exactly.\n\nThe volunteer hall… well, I still go. Or I try to. It’s funny, they changed where they keep the coffee urns now, used to be in the back cupboard near the folding chairs, and nobody ever puts things back where they belong anymore. But no, I can’t really say I’ve stopped caring about it. I just… I don’t know. I haven’t thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you're feeling irritable for much of the day, even when nothing specific is happening to bother you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day, exactly. I’m not sitting there angry from breakfast to supper. But I have been more short-tempered than I recognize in myself.\n\nWhat really made me come in was that business at the volunteer hall—I blew up at one of the younger volunteers because he’d put a tablecloth on crooked. A tablecloth. My wife used to be so particular about those things, but she’d never make someone feel small over it. And there I was raising my voice at him, and I don’t usually talk to people that way. So… yes, irritable, but I don’t know about “much of the day.”\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling irritable most days, even when nothing in particular is happening to upset you?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’m not sure I’d put it as neatly as that. I’m not angry all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut yes, I’ve been more likely to snap. What brought me in was I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth. It sounds foolish even saying it—a tablecloth. And I don’t usually talk to people that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling irritable nearly every day for most of the day, even when nothing in particular is going on to upset you?\n\nPatient: Nearly every day for most of the day… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not keeping a tally, you know.\n\nBut I have been snapping in ways that aren’t like me. That whole thing at the volunteer hall is what made me think something was off—I raised my voice at one of the younger fellows because the tablecloth was crooked. My wife always liked things set nicely, corners even and all that, but still… it was only a tablecloth. I don’t usually speak to people like that."
